Study instruction: Prepare examples illustrating bottom-up and top-down influences and explain how they interact during visual and auditory attention.
Key point:
- Bottom-up processing carries information from sensory systems towards higher-order regions.
- Top-down processing allows goals and expectations to influence sensory processing.
31. Q: What role does the prefrontal cortex play in establishing and maintaining task-related attention?
A: Show answer
The prefrontal cortex contributes to establishing task objectives, maintaining relevant information, and coordinating attentional processing according to instructions. In experiments described in the chapter, activity in the ventral prefrontal cortex increased after participants received instructions but before the relevant stimuli were presented. This preparatory activity continued until the task materials appeared. When participants were instructed to remember letters, activity was more closely associated with regions involved in verbal processing. When they were instructed to remember spatial locations, activity was more closely associated with spatial-processing regions. This suggests that the prefrontal cortex helps configure the brain for the task that is about to be performed.
Study instruction: Review the distinction between preparatory activity and stimulus-evoked activity and explain how task instructions influence subsequent processing.
Key point:
- The prefrontal cortex contributes to establishing task-related attentional priorities.
- Preparatory activity can occur before the relevant stimuli appear.
32. Q: What is set activity in the prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
Set activity refers to preparatory neural activity associated with establishing the cognitive requirements of an upcoming task. It can occur after an instruction is presented but before the relevant stimuli appear. In the chapter's example, participants were instructed either to remember a sequence of letters or to remember the locations of squares. The ventral prefrontal cortex showed sustained activity during the interval between the instruction and the presentation of the stimuli. The activity differed according to the task, suggesting that the prefrontal cortex was configuring the relevant processing system in preparation for the upcoming operation. Set activity therefore provides evidence of anticipatory attentional control.
Study instruction: Identify the timing of set activity and explain how it differs between spatial and verbal tasks.
Key point:
- Set activity reflects preparation for an upcoming cognitive task.
- It can configure the neural systems needed for subsequent processing.
33. Q: How can researchers establish whether prefrontal activity causes changes in sensory processing?
A: Show answer
Researchers need to distinguish correlation from causation. Observing that prefrontal activity is associated with increased sensory activity does not establish that the prefrontal cortex caused the change. One approach is to manipulate prefrontal activity and examine whether sensory processing changes as a consequence. The chapter describes an experiment using transcranial magnetic stimulation (TMS) to stimulate the frontal eye fields while participants underwent brain imaging. Stimulation influenced activity in higher sensory regions, with the effect depending on the task. This intervention provides stronger evidence of a causal influence than simply observing correlated activity, although conclusions must still account for the limitations of the experimental method.
Study instruction: Compare correlational neuroimaging evidence with experimental intervention and review the logic of causal inference.
Key point:
- Correlation alone does not establish causation.
- Experimental manipulation can provide stronger evidence of causal influence.
34. Q: What is transcranial magnetic stimulation, and how is it used to investigate attentional control?
A: Show answer
Transcranial magnetic stimulation is a non-invasive technique that uses magnetic pulses to influence neural activity in targeted cortical regions. In cognitive neuroscience, TMS can be used to investigate whether activity in a particular brain area contributes causally to a cognitive function. The chapter describes stimulation of the frontal eye fields while participants performed tasks involving faces and motion. Researchers observed changes in activation in sensory regions associated with the relevant task. This demonstrated that stimulation of frontal regions could influence activity in posterior sensory areas through top-down pathways. TMS therefore complements observational neuroimaging by allowing researchers to manipulate neural activity experimentally.
Study instruction: Review the basic mechanism of TMS and distinguish its experimental purpose from structural brain imaging and functional MRI.
Key point:
- TMS can experimentally influence targeted cortical activity.
- It helps investigate causal contributions to cognitive processing.
35. Q: How do top-down signals selectively enhance processing in sensory regions?
A: Show answer
Top-down signals can selectively increase activity in sensory regions that process information relevant to the current task. In the experiment described in the chapter, participants viewed faces represented by moving dots. One task required them to identify the gender of the face, while another required them to identify the direction of movement. When attention was directed towards the face, stimulation of frontal regions increased activity in the fusiform face area (FFA), which contributes to face discrimination. When attention was directed towards motion, stimulation increased activity in the MT complex, which contributes to visual motion processing. This demonstrates that top-down influences can selectively enhance different sensory systems according to task requirements.
Study instruction: Memorize the relationship between the face task, FFA, motion task, and MT complex.
Key point:
- Top-down control can selectively enhance task-relevant sensory regions.
- The specific enhancement depends on the information being attended to.
36. Q: How can attention reduce the perception of pain?
A: Show answer
Attention can influence pain perception through top-down modulation of sensory processing. The chapter describes an experiment in which participants received painful stimulation while simultaneously performing a memory task. Engaging attention in the memory task reduced activity in the sensory pathway in the spinal cord and was associated with lower reported pain. The proposed mechanism involves descending signals originating from higher-order regions, including the prefrontal cortex, that influence sensory processing. These pathways may engage endogenous opioid mechanisms that moderate pain transmission. The findings demonstrate that pain perception is influenced not only by incoming nociceptive signals but also by cognitive and attentional processes. However, distraction should not be interpreted as eliminating the underlying cause of pain or replacing appropriate medical assessment.
Study instruction: Distinguish nociceptive input from subjective pain perception and review the role of descending modulation.
Key point:
- Attention can modulate the processing and perception of pain.
- Descending pathways contribute to cognitive modulation of sensory signals.
37. Q: What are the three principal ways in which top-down signals influence attentional processing?
A: Show answer
Top-down signals influence attentional processing in three principal ways. First, they establish the task by enhancing processing in the sensory system relevant to the current objective. Second, they establish target representations or templates that allow incoming information to be compared with the desired target. Third, they help suppress processing in irrelevant sensory streams when a task must be performed in the presence of distraction. These mechanisms allow the brain to prioritize information, maintain task objectives, and reduce interference from competing stimuli. They operate together to support flexible cognitive control.
Study instruction: Memorize and explain the three mechanisms using one practical example for each.
Key point:
- Top-down signals configure relevant sensory processing.
- They establish target templates and reduce irrelevant interference.
38. Q: What is divided attention, and why is it difficult to perform multiple tasks simultaneously?
A: Show answer
Divided attention refers to the attempt to allocate cognitive resources across two or more tasks at the same time. Although the brain can perform many sensory and motor operations in parallel, certain stages of processing require shared cognitive mechanisms. When two tasks compete for these mechanisms, performance may become slower or less accurate. For example, responding to visual information while simultaneously responding to spoken words can produce interference. The difficulty does not necessarily arise because the brain cannot see and hear simultaneously. Instead, interference can occur when both tasks require overlapping decision-making and response-selection operations.
Study instruction: Distinguish parallel sensory processing from interference during simultaneous decision-making and response selection.
Key point:
- Multiple sensory operations can occur in parallel.
- Shared decision-making mechanisms can limit simultaneous task performance.
39. Q: What is dual-task interference, and how can it be measured?
A: Show answer
Dual-task interference is the reduction in performance that occurs when two tasks are performed simultaneously compared with when each task is performed separately. It can be measured by comparing reaction times, accuracy, or other performance indicators under single-task and dual-task conditions. For example, participants may be required to respond to visual shapes using different fingers while repeating words presented through headphones. If responses become slower or less accurate when both tasks are performed together, this indicates interference. The magnitude of the interference provides information about the cognitive demands and processing stages shared by the two tasks.
Study instruction: Practise calculating dual-task costs using reaction-time and accuracy data from single-task and dual-task conditions.
Key point:
- Dual-task interference indicates competition between simultaneous tasks.
- Reaction time and accuracy can be used to measure the performance cost.
40. Q: What is the central bottleneck theory of dual-task performance?
A: Show answer
The central bottleneck theory proposes that certain central cognitive operations, particularly response selection, cannot be completed simultaneously when two tasks require the same processing mechanism. Instead, one response-selection operation must be completed before the other can proceed. Sensory analysis and some motor operations may occur in parallel, but the decision stage can become a point of interference. This produces delays when individuals perform two tasks simultaneously. The theory helps explain why performing a visual/manual task while responding verbally can be slower than performing either task independently.
Study instruction: Review the central bottleneck model and identify which stages of processing may operate in parallel and which may require serial processing.
Key point:
- Central response selection can create a processing bottleneck.
- Competing tasks may have to wait for shared decision mechanisms.
41. Q: Why can individuals see and hear simultaneously but still experience difficulty responding to visual and auditory tasks together?
A: Show answer
Visual and auditory information can be analyzed through different sensory systems, allowing perception to occur in parallel. However, responding appropriately to each stimulus may require shared decision-making and response-selection processes. For example, an individual may recognize a visual shape and hear a spoken word simultaneously, but deciding which finger to move and which word to repeat can produce interference if both tasks require overlapping central processing. The difficulty therefore occurs not necessarily at the initial sensory stage but at a later stage where information must be translated into an appropriate response.
Study instruction: Separate sensory analysis, decision-making, response selection, and motor execution when explaining dual-task interference.
Key point:
- Sensory processing can be parallel across modalities.
- Central response selection can introduce serial processing limitations.
42. Q: What role does the ventral prefrontal cortex play in the central processing bottleneck?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes the ventral prefrontal cortex as a region involved in transforming sensory information into appropriate motor responses. In experiments involving visual/manual and auditory/vocal tasks, both activities engaged this region. The overlap suggests that different tasks may require shared processing mechanisms when decisions are translated into responses. This can produce interference when two tasks demand response selection at approximately the same time. However, activation in the same brain region does not by itself prove that exactly the same neurons perform both operations. The evidence supports an important role for ventral prefrontal processing in dual-task coordination, while the precise neural implementation remains a matter for further investigation.
Study instruction: Review the relationship between sensory-to-motor transformation, response selection, and dual-task interference.
Key point:
- Ventral prefrontal regions contribute to transforming sensory information into responses.
- Shared processing demands can contribute to dual-task interference.
43. Q: Why does reversing the response mappings in a dual-task experiment provide useful evidence about shared neural processing?
A: Show answer
Reversing response mappings changes the relationship between sensory input and the required response. For example, participants may initially speak in response to auditory information and move their fingers in response to visual information. The mappings can then be reversed so that visual information requires speech and auditory information requires finger movements. If both task arrangements continue to engage overlapping brain regions, this suggests that the relevant neural system may support flexible transformations between different inputs and outputs rather than being dedicated to one particular sensory or motor combination. The experiment helps investigate the generality of shared response-selection mechanisms.
Study instruction: Draw both original and reversed stimulus-response mappings and explain what continued neural overlap would imply.
Key point:
- Reversing mappings tests whether neural processing is tied to particular inputs or outputs.
- Flexible routing can help explain shared central processing demands.
44. Q: How does practice reduce interference when individuals perform two tasks simultaneously?
A: Show answer
Practice can make task performance more efficient by reducing the time and cognitive effort required to complete familiar operations. The chapter describes evidence that overtraining a visual/manual task reduces the duration of activation in the ventral prefrontal cortex. This can reduce the time for which the task occupies shared processing mechanisms and consequently reduce interference with another task. However, practice does not necessarily eliminate all dual-task limitations. Novel, unexpected, or difficult situations can still require additional decision-making and response selection, increasing the likelihood of interference.
Study instruction: Compare newly learned and overtrained tasks and explain how practice changes processing demands and dual-task costs.
Key point:
- Practice can make task processing faster and more automatic.
- Reduced processing time can decrease, but not necessarily eliminate, dual-task interference.
45. Q: What is the difference between automatic and controlled processing?
A: Show answer
Automatic processing refers to operations that have become sufficiently practiced to require relatively little conscious control and fewer central processing resources. Controlled processing involves deliberate attention, decision-making, and active management of task requirements. For example, an experienced driver may perform familiar driving operations with relatively little conscious effort, whereas responding to an unexpected obstacle requires rapid evaluation and controlled decision-making. Automaticity can improve efficiency, but it does not guarantee that a person can respond appropriately to every unexpected situation. The distinction is important when examining the limits of multitasking and the effects of distraction.
Study instruction: Identify familiar activities that become automatic through practice and compare them with situations requiring deliberate decision-making.
Key point:
- Automatic processing reduces the demand for deliberate cognitive control.
- Controlled processing is particularly important for unfamiliar or unexpected situations.
46. Q: Why can using a hands-free mobile phone while driving still be dangerous?
A: Show answer
Hands-free communication removes the need to hold a phone, but it does not eliminate the cognitive demands of conversation. Driving requires continuous monitoring of the environment, anticipation of hazards, and rapid decisions when unexpected events occur. A conversation can compete with the driver's attention and central response-selection mechanisms. Familiar driving operations may be relatively automatic, but an unexpected situation, such as a pedestrian stepping into the road, requires rapid controlled processing. If conversational demands occupy shared cognitive resources, the driver may respond more slowly or select an inappropriate action. Consequently, hands-free communication does not guarantee that driving performance will remain unaffected.
Study instruction: Explain how divided attention, automaticity, unexpected hazards, and response-selection delays interact during driving.
Key point:
- Hands-free devices do not remove cognitive distraction.
- Unexpected road events require rapid attention and decision-making.
47. Q: Why are unexpected events particularly dangerous during multitasking?
A: Show answer
Unexpected events are particularly dangerous because they require individuals to interrupt familiar or ongoing behaviour, evaluate new information, and select an appropriate response. Familiar tasks may have become automatic, reducing their usual processing demands. However, a novel event requires additional attentional and decision-making resources. If another task is occupying central processing mechanisms, the individual may experience delays in identifying the hazard and choosing an appropriate response. During driving, for example, a pedestrian suddenly entering the road requires immediate evaluation and selection of an avoidance manoeuvre. Even a relatively short response delay can have serious consequences.
Study instruction: Analyse an unexpected-event scenario and identify the stages at which attentional and response-selection delays may occur.
Key point:
- Novel events increase demands on controlled processing.
- Competing tasks can delay responses to unexpected hazards.
48. Q: What is the difference between task switching and divided attention?
A: Show answer
Divided attention involves attempting to perform multiple tasks within the same period, whereas task switching involves moving between different tasks rather than maintaining both continuously. Both can create performance costs, but their mechanisms are not identical. Dual-task interference may arise when two activities compete for shared processing stages. Task-switching costs may arise because the individual must reconfigure task goals, retrieve relevant rules, and adjust attentional priorities. For example, alternating between writing a report and responding to emails involves task switching, while simultaneously listening to a conversation and responding to a visual signal involves divided attention.
Study instruction: Prepare examples distinguishing simultaneous dual-task performance from sequential task switching.
Key point:
- Divided attention concerns overlapping task performance.
- Task switching concerns changing from one task to another.
49. Q: How can attention be investigated through behavioural experiments and brain imaging?
A: Show answer
Behavioural experiments investigate attention by measuring performance indicators such as reaction time, accuracy, missed targets, and eye movements. Brain imaging methods such as functional magnetic resonance imaging identify changes in neural activity associated with different attentional conditions. Researchers can compare activity when participants attend to a stimulus with activity when they ignore it, or examine differences between task conditions. More advanced approaches, including multivoxel pattern analysis, investigate the information represented in distributed patterns of neural activity. Combining behavioural measurements with neuroimaging provides a more comprehensive understanding of how attentional mechanisms relate to observable performance.
Study instruction: Compare the research questions addressed by reaction-time experiments, eye tracking, functional MRI, and MVPA.
Key point:
- Behavioural methods measure observable attentional performance.
- Neuroimaging methods investigate the neural activity associated with attention.
50. Q: What are the principal limitations of interpreting brain activation as evidence of a specific cognitive function?
A: Show answer
Brain activation does not automatically establish that a particular region is exclusively responsible for a cognitive function. The same region may participate in multiple operations, and complex behaviour usually depends on interactions between distributed neural systems. Increased activation may reflect several processes, including sensory analysis, response preparation, attentional control, or eye-movement planning. Similarly, correlated activity between two regions does not establish that one causes the other to become active. Researchers should therefore combine behavioural evidence, functional connectivity, anatomical information, experimental manipulation, and clinical observations before drawing conclusions about neural function.
Study instruction: Review the distinction between association, functional specialization, network participation, and causal evidence.
Key point:
- A brain region's activation does not prove that it performs only one function.
- Strong explanations require converging evidence from multiple research methods.
51. Q: How do attentional networks contribute to the distinction between relevant and irrelevant information?
A: Show answer
Attentional networks coordinate the selection of information according to current goals and environmental demands. The dorsal network helps maintain priority for task-relevant locations and features, while the ventral network contributes to detecting important events that require attention to be redirected. Prefrontal regions influence sensory processing through top-down signals, enhancing relevant representations and reducing the influence of irrelevant information. Temporal visual regions contribute to identifying objects, while parietal regions support spatial selection. The combined activity of these systems enables the brain to distinguish targets from distractors in complex environments.
Study instruction: Construct a process diagram connecting task goals, attentional networks, sensory enhancement, distractor suppression, and target detection.
Key point:
- Attentional selection depends on interactions between multiple neural systems.
- Goal-directed enhancement and distractor suppression improve information selection.
52. Q: How can selective attention, spatial neglect, and dual-task interference be understood within one integrated framework?
A: Show answer
These phenomena demonstrate different aspects of the organization and limitations of attentional control. Selective attention shows how the brain prioritizes relevant information and reduces interference from irrelevant stimuli. Spatial neglect demonstrates how damage to interconnected attentional networks can produce systematic biases in spatial awareness and target detection. Dual-task interference shows that even though sensory and motor operations can occur in parallel, certain central cognitive processes have limited capacity and may need to operate sequentially. Together, these findings indicate that effective behaviour depends on distributed neural systems that coordinate sensory processing, spatial selection, task preparation, decision-making, and response execution. Attention is therefore neither a single brain function nor a simple limitation in the total processing capacity of the brain.
Study instruction: Integrate the chapter's major concepts into one explanation covering selective attention, dorsal and ventral networks, top-down control, spatial neglect, and dual-task limitations.
Key point:
- Attentional control depends on coordinated neural networks and task demands.
- Different attentional limitations arise at different stages of cognitive processing.
