80. Q: How can brain development and plasticity influence intellectual abilities?
A: Show answer
Brain development involves changes in neural structure, connectivity, and functional specialization. Experience, learning, and environmental conditions influence how neural systems develop and operate. Plasticity allows neural networks to adapt through experience, although the extent and nature of adaptation vary across developmental stages and cognitive systems. These processes help explain why intellectual abilities are influenced by both biological characteristics and environmental opportunities. They also demonstrate why cognitive development should not be understood as a fixed outcome determined exclusively by genetic inheritance.
Study instruction:
- Review the difference between neural development and plasticity and identify examples of experience-dependent cognitive development.
Key point:
- Brain organization changes during development and through experience.
- Biological capacities interact with learning environments.
81. Q: What is the difference between evolutionary expansion and developmental growth of the brain?
A: Show answer
Evolutionary expansion refers to changes in brain structure across generations within a species lineage. Developmental growth refers to changes occurring within an individual during their lifetime, including increases in neural size, changes in connectivity, and functional specialization. These processes are related but operate at different timescales. Evolutionary changes can alter the biological characteristics and developmental processes of a species, while individual development is influenced by inherited characteristics and environmental experience.
Study instruction:
- Compare evolutionary changes across primate species with neural development during childhood and adolescence.
Key point:
- Evolutionary expansion occurs across generations.
- Individual brain development occurs during a person's lifetime.
82. Q: How does comparative neuroscience help explain human intelligence?
A: Show answer
Comparative neuroscience examines similarities and differences between the nervous systems of humans and other species. It investigates brain structure, regional proportions, connectivity, development, and cognitive behavior. Comparing humans with chimpanzees and other primates helps identify evolutionary changes associated with human-specific cognitive characteristics. These comparisons can reveal whether a feature reflects general primate organization or a more specialized evolutionary development. However, conclusions must consider differences in brain size, developmental timing, ecology, and the limitations of comparing cognitive performance across species.
Study instruction:
- Compare human and non-human primate brain research and identify the evidence needed to support an evolutionary explanation of cognitive differences.
Key point:
- Comparative neuroscience identifies evolutionary similarities and differences.
- Species comparisons require careful interpretation of anatomical and behavioral evidence.
83. Q: What is the significance of association cortex in the evolution of human cognition?
A: Show answer
Association cortex integrates information from multiple sensory, motor, memory, and cognitive systems. It supports higher-order activities such as abstraction, planning, categorization, language, and complex reasoning. Its evolutionary expansion contributed to the increased capacity to combine information from different sources and apply it flexibly. The significance lies not simply in having more cortical tissue but in the development of systems capable of coordinating complex information and supporting sophisticated behavior.
Study instruction:
- Explain how association cortex differs functionally from primary sensory and motor cortex using examples of integrated cognitive tasks.
Key point:
- Association cortex supports higher-order information integration.
- Its expansion contributed to important aspects of human cognitive specialization.
84. Q: How does human intelligence depend on the integration of knowledge and reasoning?
A: Show answer
Knowledge supplies information about the world, while reasoning allows individuals to identify relationships, evaluate evidence, and derive new conclusions. Knowledge without reasoning may result in the memorization of facts without effective application. Reasoning without relevant knowledge may be insufficient when a problem requires understanding specialized information. Human intelligence depends on combining both capacities. Language and education provide accumulated knowledge, while frontoparietal systems help manipulate and apply information in unfamiliar situations.
Study instruction:
- Select a practical problem and identify which elements require previously acquired knowledge and which require new reasoning.
Key point:
- Knowledge and reasoning are distinct but complementary.
- Effective problem-solving requires both relevant information and the ability to manipulate it.
85. Q: Why is abstract similarity an important component of human intelligence?
A: Show answer
Abstract similarity involves recognizing that two relationships or structures are equivalent even when their individual elements differ. For example, a painter uses a brush and a writer uses a pen. The objects are different, but their functional relationships are comparable. This ability allows individuals to transfer knowledge from one context to another, identify common principles, and understand abstract categories. It is important for scientific reasoning, mathematics, education, and problem-solving because it allows previously learned structures to be applied to unfamiliar situations.
Study instruction:
- Develop analogies from different subject areas and explain the shared abstract relationship in each example.
Key point:
- Abstract similarity enables transfer of relational knowledge.
- Analogical reasoning is important for flexible problem-solving.
86. Q: How can the brain solve a problem presented in a form that differs from previously learned examples?
A: Show answer
The brain can solve unfamiliar problems by identifying underlying relationships rather than relying exclusively on memorized examples. The individual examines the available information, identifies relevant patterns, formulates possible rules, and evaluates whether those rules consistently explain the problem. Executive-control systems help maintain the task objective and coordinate successive operations, while parietal systems contribute to representing relationships. This flexibility is a central characteristic of fluid reasoning and allows previously developed cognitive processes to be applied in new situations.
Study instruction:
- Solve unfamiliar problems using more than one representation and explain how identifying underlying relationships makes transfer possible.
Key point:
- Flexible reasoning depends on identifying transferable relationships.
- Executive control helps organize the application of unfamiliar rules.
87. Q: What are the limitations of using neuroimaging to study human intelligence?
A: Show answer
Neuroimaging provides information about brain activity and functional relationships during cognitive tasks, but it does not directly reveal every mental process involved. The interpretation depends on experimental design, task difficulty, participant characteristics, and the method used to compare activation patterns. An active region may participate in several functions, and the same cognitive task may recruit different combinations of regions depending on its demands. Neuroimaging findings should therefore be interpreted alongside behavioral assessments, lesion evidence, and other research methods.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a published functional imaging study by identifying its task, control condition, measured outcome, and limitations.
Key point:
- Neuroimaging provides indirect evidence about cognitive processes.
- Multiple research methods are necessary for strong conclusions.
88. Q: How should evolutionary explanations of human intelligence be evaluated?
A: Show answer
Evolutionary explanations should be evaluated using converging evidence from comparative anatomy, genetics, developmental neuroscience, fossil evidence, behavioral research, and the study of living species. Researchers should distinguish observed anatomical differences from hypotheses about their functional consequences. They should also avoid assuming that every human-specific feature evolved directly for intelligence or that brain expansion has a single explanation. Modern evolutionary neuroscience recognizes that cortical and subcortical systems, developmental mechanisms, ecological pressures, and cultural processes all contribute to understanding human cognitive evolution.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate an evolutionary neuroscience claim and identify the anatomical, developmental, comparative, and behavioral evidence needed to support it.
Key point:
- Evolutionary explanations require multiple forms of evidence.
- Anatomical differences should not automatically be treated as proof of specific cognitive advantages.
89. Q: How do reasoning, language, cultural inheritance, and brain evolution combine to explain human intelligence?
A: Show answer
Human intelligence reflects the interaction of several complementary processes. Evolved neural systems support executive control, semantic knowledge, spatial representation, language, and relational reasoning. Language enables communication and the transmission of accumulated knowledge. Cultural inheritance allows individuals to build on discoveries and skills developed by previous generations. Executive and relational systems enable this knowledge to be analyzed, reorganized, and applied to unfamiliar problems. Human intelligence therefore cannot be explained by language, brain size, education, or any single neural region independently. It emerges from the interaction of biological, cognitive, developmental, and cultural processes.
Study instruction:
- Develop an integrated explanation connecting neural specialization, fluid reasoning, language, education, and cumulative cultural knowledge.
Key point:
- Human intelligence depends on interacting biological and cultural systems.
- Advanced cognition combines accumulated knowledge with flexible reasoning.
90. Q: What are the most important conclusions about reasoning and human intelligence from Chapter 5?
A: Show answer
Chapter 5 presents three central conclusions. First, different intellectual abilities tend to correlate because they depend partly on shared frontoparietal executive systems, contributing to general intelligence. Second, logical reasoning does not necessarily require inner speech: language is important for communication and knowledge acquisition, but thought can operate through semantic, spatial, and relational representations. Third, human intelligence reflects the interaction of specialized and expanded cortical systems with cumulative cultural inheritance. Language allows knowledge to be transmitted across generations, while evolved neural networks support the analysis, organization, and application of that knowledge. Together, these findings explain why human intelligence depends on both biological capacities and accumulated cultural understanding.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a structured explanation of the chapter covering general intelligence, the neural basis of reasoning, the relationship between language and thought, and the evolutionary development of human cognitive abilities.
Key point:
- General intelligence is associated with shared executive and frontoparietal systems.
- Reasoning can occur without inner speech.
- Human intelligence depends on evolved neural specialization and cumulative cultural inheritance.

Table 6
Decision-Making, Executive Control, and the Neuroscience of Human Judgment
1. Q: Why do we sometimes make absent-minded mistakes?
A: Show answer
Absent-minded mistakes occur when habitual behaviour continues even though the circumstances or intended goal have changed. The prefrontal cortex helps coordinate information about the current environment, personal needs, intended outcomes, and appropriate actions. When an action has been repeated frequently, it can become automatic and require less conscious supervision. This is efficient when circumstances remain predictable but creates problems when the situation changes. For example, someone intending to drive to the supermarket may automatically follow the familiar route to work because the habitual sequence is activated without adequately checking the current intention. Donald Broadbent described such everyday lapses as cognitive failures. These mistakes do not necessarily indicate low intelligence; they can reflect a failure to re-engage attentive control when circumstances change.
Study instruction:
- Review the distinction between attentive control and habitual behaviour, particularly the role of the prefrontal cortex in adjusting actions to changing circumstances.
Key point:
- Habitual actions can continue automatically after their original purpose has changed.
- Absent-mindedness can result from insufficient monitoring of current intentions.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- Neural Substrates of Habit
2. Q: What is decision-making from a neuroscience perspective?
A: Show answer
Decision-making is the process through which an individual evaluates available alternatives and selects an action based on the current situation, anticipated outcomes, personal objectives, and potential consequences. It involves several interacting brain systems rather than one isolated decision-making centre. The prefrontal cortex integrates information about the external environment, internal needs, expected rewards, possible risks, and available actions. The ventromedial prefrontal cortex contributes to representing subjective value, while dorsal prefrontal regions contribute to planning, maintaining intentions, and controlling behaviour. Other regions, including the striatum, amygdala, and parietal cortex, contribute to learning, emotional significance, and the evaluation of alternatives. Decision-making therefore involves transforming information into goal-directed behaviour.
Study instruction:
- Identify the major brain regions involved in evaluating alternatives, planning actions, and selecting behaviour.
Key point:
- Decision-making depends on coordinated neural networks.
- Decisions integrate information, goals, values, and anticipated consequences.
Source links:
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
3. Q: What is the prefrontal cortex, and why is it important for decision-making?
A: Show answer
The prefrontal cortex is the anterior portion of the frontal lobes and is involved in higher-order cognitive functions, including planning, reasoning, working memory, behavioural regulation, and decision-making. Its importance arises partly from its extensive connections with sensory, emotional, motivational, and motor systems. It receives information about the external environment, communicates with systems involved in hunger, thirst, and other internal needs, and influences motor areas responsible for implementing actions. Through these connections, it can evaluate the current situation, compare possible responses, and select behaviour that is consistent with current objectives. Its different subdivisions contribute to different aspects of decision-making rather than performing one identical function.
Study instruction:
- Review the anatomical connections of the prefrontal cortex and explain how sensory information, internal needs, and motor systems contribute to decisions.
Key point:
- The prefrontal cortex integrates information necessary for flexible, goal-directed behaviour.
- Its function depends on communication with other brain regions.
Source links:
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
4. Q: How does the prefrontal cortex receive information about the external environment?
A: Show answer
Different sensory pathways provide the prefrontal cortex with information about the surrounding world. Visual information reaches it through pathways involving the inferior temporal cortex. Auditory information comes through the superior temporal cortex. Somatosensory information, including touch, is transmitted through parietal sensory regions, while information about visual space involves the inferior parietal cortex. These inputs allow the prefrontal cortex to construct a multimodal representation of the current situation. Combining information from several sensory modalities enables individuals to select actions based on the overall context rather than responding independently to isolated sensory signals. For example, crossing a road requires integrating visual information about approaching vehicles, auditory information about traffic, and an assessment of the available time to cross.
Study instruction:
- Trace the major sensory pathways supplying the prefrontal cortex and consider how combining sensory information improves context-dependent behaviour.
Key point:
- The prefrontal cortex receives information from multiple sensory systems.
- Multimodal integration supports appropriate responses to complex situations.
Source links:
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
5. Q: How does the prefrontal cortex incorporate internal needs and motivations into decisions?
A: Show answer
Decision-making requires consideration of both external circumstances and internal physiological or motivational conditions. The orbital prefrontal cortex is interconnected with the amygdala, which communicates with the hypothalamus. The hypothalamus contributes to regulating physiological conditions such as hunger, thirst, and temperature. Information about these internal conditions influences how attractive or necessary particular outcomes become. For example, food may have considerable subjective value when someone is hungry but substantially less value after a large meal. The same external opportunity can therefore produce different decisions depending on the individual's current physiological and motivational state. This demonstrates why decision-making cannot be understood solely by examining external rewards.
Study instruction:
- Examine how the amygdala, hypothalamus, and orbital prefrontal cortex contribute to the relationship between physiological needs and behavioural choices.
Key point:
- Internal needs influence the subjective value of available outcomes.
- Decision-making changes as physiological and motivational conditions change.
Source links:
- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
6. Q: What is the difference between the ventral and dorsal prefrontal cortex in decision-making?
A: Show answer
The ventral and dorsal prefrontal regions contribute to different but interconnected aspects of decision-making. Ventral prefrontal regions are particularly important for evaluating sensory information, representing subjective values, and relating potential outcomes to the current situation. The ventromedial portion contributes to integrating reward and value information. Dorsal prefrontal regions contribute more directly to cognitive control, generating actions, maintaining intentions, preparing sequences, and planning future behaviour. These functions are not completely separated because the subdivisions communicate extensively. For example, choosing whether to accept a job involves evaluating its expected benefits and personal value while also planning the actions necessary to pursue it. The evaluation and planning processes must cooperate to produce an effective decision.
Study instruction:
- Compare the functions of ventral, ventromedial, and dorsal prefrontal regions without assuming that each operates independently.
Key point:
- Ventral systems contribute substantially to value evaluation.
- Dorsal systems contribute substantially to planning and cognitive control.
Source links:
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
7. Q: What does it mean to banish the homunculus when explaining decision-making?
A: Show answer
The homunculus is a hypothetical miniature person imagined to exist inside the brain and somehow responsible for controlling behaviour. This explanation creates an additional problem because it does not explain how the supposed internal controller makes decisions. If another decision-maker is required to control the first one, the same problem simply reappears. Neuroscience avoids this problem by explaining decision-making through identifiable information-processing mechanisms, neural connections, and interactions between brain regions. Instead of claiming that the prefrontal cortex independently makes decisions, researchers investigate how sensory information, motivational signals, value representations, and motor systems interact to generate behaviour. This approach replaces an unexplained internal controller with a scientifically testable account of neural processes.
Study instruction:
- Explain why attributing decisions to a single brain region is insufficient without identifying the information-processing mechanisms involved.
Key point:
- The brain does not require a miniature internal decision-maker.
- Decision-making must be explained through interacting neural processes.
Source links:
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
8. Q: How does the brain compare the values of different choices?
A: Show answer
The brain compares choices by representing their subjective value in a way that permits different types of outcomes to be evaluated against one another. This is particularly important when alternatives provide different forms of reward. For example, a person may need to choose between purchasing food, clothing, or entertainment. These outcomes cannot be compared simply by measuring their physical characteristics. Instead, their relative attractiveness depends on individual preferences, current needs, and expected benefits. Research has identified value-related activity in the ventromedial prefrontal cortex that is associated with subjective evaluations across different reward categories. This is sometimes described as a common neural currency because different outcomes can be represented on a shared subjective-value dimension. The representation does not imply that every reward is experienced identically; it enables relative comparison during choice.
Study instruction:
- Review the concept of common neural currency and distinguish subjective value from the physical intensity of a reward.
Key point:
- Different rewards can be evaluated using a shared subjective-value framework.
- Individual preferences and current needs influence the value assigned to alternatives.
Source links:
- Economic and Social Values in the Brain: Evidence from Lesions to the Human Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
- Specializations for Reward-Guided Decision-Making in the Primate Ventral Prefrontal Cortex
9. Q: What is the difference between objective reward and subjective value?
A: Show answer
Objective reward refers to measurable characteristics of an outcome, such as its monetary amount, quantity, or physical properties. Subjective value refers to how attractive or beneficial that outcome is to a particular individual under particular circumstances. A $50 reward has a measurable monetary value, but its subjective importance may differ between people depending on their financial circumstances, preferences, and needs. Similarly, a glass of water may have high subjective value to someone who is thirsty and little immediate value to someone who is fully hydrated. The distinction matters because the brain's value-related activity is often associated with subjective pleasantness or desirability rather than simply the physical intensity or objective magnitude of a stimulus.
Study instruction:
- Practise distinguishing measurable reward characteristics from individual evaluations of those rewards.
Key point:
- Objective reward and subjective value are not interchangeable.
- The same reward can have different values for different people or at different times.
Source links:
- Economic and Social Values in the Brain: Evidence from Lesions to the Human Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
10. Q: How does the ventromedial prefrontal cortex contribute to comparing different types of reward?
A: Show answer
The ventromedial prefrontal cortex contributes to representing the subjective value of different outcomes. Research comparing sensory rewards, such as pleasant taste and warmth, has identified neural activity associated with how pleasant participants consider those experiences. The strength of the value-related signal can correspond to subjective pleasantness rather than simply the physical intensity of the stimulation. This provides evidence that the brain can evaluate different kinds of experiences using a common value-related representation. The same general mechanism can contribute to choices involving food, money, social approval, and other desirable outcomes. However, the ventromedial prefrontal cortex operates within a broader network, and its precise contribution varies with the type of decision and the stage of value processing.
Study instruction:
- Review research comparing different sensory rewards and explain why subjective ratings are important when interpreting neural activity.
Key point:
- Ventromedial prefrontal activity is associated with subjective reward value.
- Different categories of reward can engage overlapping valuation processes.
Source links:
- Economic and Social Values in the Brain: Evidence from Lesions to the Human Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
11. Q: What is attentive control, and how does it differ from habitual control?
A: Show answer
Attentive control refers to behaviour that is actively guided by current circumstances, intentions, and goals. It allows individuals to modify their actions when the environment or their objectives change. Habitual control refers to behaviour that is performed automatically after repeated practice. Habitual actions require fewer cognitive resources because the individual does not need to reconsider every step. For example, a person who regularly drives to work may automatically follow the familiar route. Attentive control becomes particularly important when that person must travel somewhere different. Effective behaviour requires both systems: habits provide efficiency in predictable circumstances, while attentive control provides flexibility in changing situations.
Study instruction:
- Compare goal-directed and habitual behaviour using examples from driving, work routines, and everyday activities.
Key point:
- Attentive control supports flexible behaviour.
- Habitual control supports efficient repetition of learned actions.
Source links:
- Neural Substrates of Habit
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
12. Q: Why do habitual actions become automatic after repeated practice?
A: Show answer
Repeated practice strengthens the associations between situations, actions, and subsequent movements. During initial learning, individuals must consciously monitor their behaviour, evaluate feedback, and determine which action should occur next. This requires substantial engagement of cognitive-control systems, including the prefrontal cortex. As practice continues, learned sequences can increasingly depend on interconnected motor and striatal circuits. These circuits allow one movement to cue the next, reducing the need for conscious supervision of every action. The resulting automaticity improves efficiency and reduces cognitive demands. However, automaticity can become problematic when a familiar action is performed in a situation where it is no longer appropriate.
Study instruction:
- Review how repeated practice changes the neural systems supporting behaviour and distinguish initial learning from established habit performance.
Key point:
- Repetition can reduce the cognitive demands of performing learned sequences.
- Automaticity improves efficiency but can reduce sensitivity to changing circumstances.
Source links:
- Neural Substrates of Habit
13. Q: What role does the striatum play in habit formation?
A: Show answer
The striatum is a group of subcortical structures involved in action selection, reinforcement learning, and the development of habitual behaviour. Its different subdivisions contribute to different aspects of behavioural control. The posterior striatum is particularly associated with learned action sequences and established stimulus-response relationships. During repeated practice, activity can become concentrated in circuits involving the posterior striatum, medial parietal cortex, supplementary motor area, and motor cortex. These interconnected regions allow one movement to trigger the next without requiring extensive conscious planning. The striatum therefore contributes to the transition from flexible, effortful action selection towards more automatic performance. Habit formation nevertheless depends on interactions between cortical and subcortical systems.
Study instruction:
- Identify the striatum's contribution to action selection and distinguish the neural mechanisms supporting habit formation from those supporting deliberate planning.
Key point:
- The striatum contributes to learning and executing habitual actions.
- Habitual behaviour involves interconnected cortical and subcortical circuits.
Source links:
- Neural Substrates of Habit
14. Q: What did Ivan Toni's sequence-learning experiment demonstrate about the prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
Ivan Toni's experiment examined how brain activity changes when individuals learn a sequence of actions through trial and error. Participants learned an eight-action sequence by pressing keys at intervals and receiving feedback indicating whether each response was correct. During initial learning, the dorsal prefrontal cortex showed widespread activation because participants had to identify the correct order of actions. After approximately 45 minutes of training, this activation had almost disappeared. The reduction indicated that the sequence had become sufficiently familiar for its execution to require less conscious control. The experiment demonstrated that prefrontal involvement is particularly important during the acquisition of unfamiliar action sequences and can decline as performance becomes automatic.
Study instruction:
- Memorise the experimental design, the initial pattern of prefrontal activation, and the change observed after practice.
Key point:
- Initial trial-and-error learning requires substantial cognitive control.
- Repeated practice can reduce prefrontal involvement in sequence execution.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
15. Q: How does the brain respond when a familiar habitual sequence is no longer appropriate?
A: Show answer
When a familiar sequence becomes inappropriate, attentive control must be re-engaged so that behaviour can be adjusted to the current situation. The dorsal prefrontal cortex contributes to this process by helping individuals maintain their intentions and prepare appropriate actions. For example, someone who normally drives to work may need to change direction because they are going to a different destination. If habitual control dominates, the familiar route may continue automatically. Deliberately considering the current destination can help restore attentive control. Research discussed in the chapter showed that simply asking participants to think about an otherwise repetitive sequence was sufficient to re-engage dorsal prefrontal activity.
Study instruction:
- Explain how changes in context and deliberate attention can interrupt an established action sequence.
Key point:
- Habitual behaviour must be monitored when circumstances change.
- Re-engaging attentive control helps prevent inappropriate automatic actions.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
16. Q: What are cognitive failures, and what causes them?
A: Show answer
Cognitive failures are everyday errors involving attention, memory, or the control of intended actions. They can occur when automatic behaviour is executed without sufficient consideration of current intentions or environmental changes. Examples include entering a familiar room to perform a task but forgetting the purpose of the visit, driving towards an old destination, or carrying out a routine action at the wrong time. Such failures can arise because habitual action sequences are activated more strongly than the intention that should guide current behaviour. They are not necessarily evidence of a general memory disorder or reduced intelligence. Their frequency and significance depend on the circumstances, task demands, and effectiveness of attentional control.
Study instruction:
- Identify everyday examples of cognitive failures and explain whether each involves attention, prospective memory, habitual behaviour, or a combination of these mechanisms.
Key point:
- Cognitive failures can arise from inadequate control of automatic actions.
- Maintaining awareness of current intentions helps prevent everyday errors.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
17. Q: What is the difference between externally guided and internally generated actions?
A: Show answer
Externally guided actions are selected in response to instructions or cues provided by the environment. For example, a light may indicate which button a participant should press. Internally generated actions require the individual to determine what to do without an immediate external instruction specifying the response. For example, participants may be asked to generate a sequence of key presses while avoiding repeated use of the same key. This requires them to maintain the task objective, monitor previous actions, and decide which action should come next. Internally generated behaviour places greater demands on cognitive control and is associated with increased engagement of dorsal prefrontal regions.
Study instruction:
- Compare externally instructed actions with internally generated sequences and identify the additional cognitive demands involved in the latter.
Key point:
- External cues can specify the required response.
- Internally generated actions require active selection and monitoring.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
18. Q: What did James Rowe's internally generated sequence experiment demonstrate?
A: Show answer
James Rowe and colleagues investigated the brain activity associated with producing internally generated sequences of key presses. Participants had to generate sequences while avoiding long runs of the same response. The dorsal prefrontal cortex was strongly activated during this condition. Importantly, activation increased as the number of switches between keys increased. It was not present in the same way on the first trial, when there was no established sequence and therefore no switching requirement. These findings suggest that dorsal prefrontal activation was associated with the attentive control required to generate and modify flexible behaviour rather than simply producing movements. The experiment demonstrated the importance of the prefrontal cortex in internally controlled action selection.
Study instruction:
- Review the experimental condition, the relationship between switching frequency and activation, and the implications for flexible behavioural control.
Key point:
- Internally generated sequences require active cognitive control.
- Dorsal prefrontal activity is associated with flexible action selection and switching.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
19. Q: What is working memory, and how is it different from short-term memory?
A: Show answer
Short-term memory refers primarily to retaining information over a relatively brief period. Working memory involves maintaining information while using it to perform an ongoing or forthcoming cognitive task. For example, remembering a telephone number for several seconds involves short-term retention, whereas remembering the number while rearranging its digits or preparing to enter it involves working-memory processes. In the context of prefrontal function, it is particularly important to distinguish simply retaining information from maintaining information in a form that supports preparation and action. The prefrontal cortex contributes to working-memory-related control, although short-term retention can depend on other regions, including the parietal cortex. Working memory is therefore not equivalent to passive storage.
Study instruction:
- Practise distinguishing temporary information retention from the active maintenance and manipulation of information required for a task.
Key point:
- Short-term memory emphasises temporary retention.
- Working memory supports the use of temporarily maintained information to guide cognition and behaviour.
Source links:
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
20. Q: Why is the prefrontal cortex associated with preparing future actions rather than simply retaining information?
A: Show answer
Research comparing memory-retention and action-preparation conditions suggests that prefrontal activity can reflect the intention to perform a future action rather than the passive retention of information. In an experiment described by Jean-Baptiste Pochon and Bruno Dubois, participants viewed sequences of lights and were subsequently asked to recognise or reproduce them after a delay. During simple recognition, continuous delay-period activation was observed in the parietal cortex rather than the prefrontal cortex. When participants had to prepare to reproduce the sequence, dorsal prefrontal activation appeared during the delay. This suggests that the prefrontal cortex contributes particularly to prospective preparation and task-directed control.
Study instruction:
- Compare the recognition and reproduction conditions and explain why prospective preparation produces different neural demands from passive retention.
Key point:
- Prefrontal activation can reflect preparation for future action.
- Retention and preparation should not automatically be treated as identical cognitive processes.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
21. Q: What is prospective processing, and how does it differ from retrospective processing?
A: Show answer
Retrospective processing involves information about previous events or experiences, whereas prospective processing concerns intended future actions and outcomes. In cognitive neuroscience, this distinction helps explain why maintaining information does not necessarily mean preparing to use it. Someone may remember a sequence of instructions without intending to act on them immediately. Alternatively, they may retain the same instructions specifically because they are preparing to execute them. The second condition introduces an additional requirement: the information must remain available in a form that supports future behaviour. Dorsal prefrontal regions contribute to such preparation, particularly when an individual must organise or generate actions after a delay.
Study instruction:
- Develop examples that distinguish remembering what happened from maintaining an intention to do something later.
Key point:
- Retrospective processing concerns previous information.
- Prospective processing supports future-directed action and preparation.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
22. Q: What is the difference between waiting to act and planning?
A: Show answer
Waiting to act means maintaining an intention or prepared response until an appropriate opportunity arises. Planning involves actively determining which actions should be performed, in what order, and how they will achieve a desired outcome. For example, waiting for a traffic light to turn green involves maintaining readiness to move. Planning a journey involves selecting a destination, identifying possible routes, considering obstacles, and determining the sequence of actions required. Planning therefore involves more extensive prospective reasoning and evaluation of alternatives. The dorsal prefrontal cortex contributes to generating action sequences, while other regions, including the intraparietal sulcus, contribute to evaluating spatial or task-related relationships between actions and goals.
Study instruction:
- Compare situations involving delayed execution with situations requiring the construction of a new action plan.
Key point:
- Waiting involves maintaining readiness for action.
- Planning involves constructing and evaluating a sequence of actions.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
23. Q: What is the Tower of London task, and what does it measure?
A: Show answer
The Tower of London task is a laboratory assessment of planning and problem-solving. Participants are shown an initial arrangement of objects, traditionally coloured balls, and a target arrangement. They must determine the sequence of moves needed to transform the starting arrangement into the target arrangement, often while following restrictions such as moving one object at a time. Some problems can be solved in relatively few moves, while others require more complex sequences and advance consideration of intermediate positions. The task measures aspects of planning, action sequencing, and the ability to anticipate the consequences of possible moves. It provides a controlled way of studying cognitive processes involved in organising future behaviour.
Study instruction:
- Practise solving Tower of London problems and explain why identifying intermediate moves is necessary for reaching the final arrangement efficiently.
Key point:
- The task assesses planning and sequential problem-solving.
- Effective performance requires anticipating the consequences of possible actions.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
24. Q: Which brain regions are involved in solving Tower of London problems?
A: Show answer
Research by James Rowe and Adrian Owen identified activation in the dorsal prefrontal cortex and the intraparietal sulcus during Tower of London problem-solving. These regions contribute to different aspects of the task. Dorsal prefrontal activation is associated with generating and organising the sequence of moves needed to reach the target arrangement. The intraparietal sulcus contributes to evaluating spatial relationships and checking proposed moves against the goal configuration. Although these areas are anatomically connected, their contributions are distinguishable. Effective planning therefore requires both generating a possible course of action and evaluating whether that course of action will achieve the intended outcome.
Study instruction:
- Review the functional distinction between action-sequence generation and spatial evaluation in planning tasks.
Key point:
- The dorsal prefrontal cortex contributes to generating plans.
- The intraparietal sulcus contributes to evaluating moves against the goal.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
25. Q: Why can someone perform well on a simple planning test but struggle with complex real-life planning?
A: Show answer
Simple laboratory tasks often place relatively limited demands on planning because they restrict the number of objects, possible actions, and relevant environmental conditions. Real-life planning requires coordinating many more variables, including competing objectives, changing circumstances, incomplete information, social expectations, and multiple stages of execution. Someone may successfully solve a relatively simple Tower of London problem but struggle to organise a complex project involving several people, deadlines, resources, and dependencies. The difference illustrates why laboratory performance cannot automatically establish that an individual has intact planning abilities in everyday life. Comprehensive assessment should consider both controlled experimental tasks and practical performance in realistic situations.
Study instruction:
- Compare the cognitive demands of laboratory planning tasks with those of complex everyday activities.
Key point:
- Simple task performance does not necessarily demonstrate effective real-world planning.
- Complex environments require integrating multiple goals and constraints.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
26. Q: What did the case of patient PF demonstrate about the importance of the prefrontal cortex in planning?
A: Show answer
Patient PF had undergone removal of a tumour from the right frontal lobe. Although he could perform a relatively simple Tower of London task, he struggled with more complex planning activities. His difficulty became particularly apparent when he was asked to redesign a laboratory. His preliminary proposals were disorganised, lacked detail, and consisted of disconnected ideas. He was unable to develop these initial proposals into a coherent and workable plan. Before his operation, PF had worked as an architect, but afterwards he could no longer maintain that professional role. The case demonstrated that relatively preserved intelligence and performance on simple tests do not guarantee the ability to organise complex real-world activities. It also illustrates the importance of studying functional outcomes following brain injury.
Study instruction:
- Review the patient's performance on laboratory tasks and the practical redesign exercise, focusing on the difference between simple and complex planning.
Key point:
- Prefrontal damage can impair complex everyday planning.
- Conventional intelligence and simple task performance may not reveal every executive deficit.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Role of Prefrontal Cortex in Cognitive Control and Executive Function
27. Q: What is mental trial and error, and why is it important for decision-making?
A: Show answer
Mental trial and error involves imagining alternative actions and evaluating their possible consequences before carrying them out. Instead of physically attempting every available option, individuals can simulate potential outcomes mentally and eliminate actions that are likely to produce undesirable results. The prefrontal cortex contributes to generating and evaluating these prospective possibilities. For example, before accepting a new job, someone might consider the consequences for income, family commitments, professional development, and future opportunities. This ability reduces the need to learn entirely through actual experience. Its evolutionary advantage is that individuals can anticipate potentially harmful outcomes and adjust their behaviour before committing to an action.
Study instruction:
- Apply mental trial and error to a realistic decision and identify how imagining consequences can eliminate unsuitable alternatives.
Key point:
- Mental simulation allows individuals to evaluate actions before performing them.
- Anticipating consequences can reduce avoidable mistakes.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
28. Q: Why do people sometimes prefer smaller immediate rewards over larger future rewards?
A: Show answer
People often prefer immediate rewards because future outcomes are represented less vividly and may feel less psychologically compelling than rewards available now. Mental representations of future experiences are not necessarily as detailed or emotionally engaging as direct experiences. This can reduce the subjective attractiveness of delayed rewards. For example, someone may choose $25 immediately instead of waiting to receive a larger amount later, even when the delayed option provides greater total financial benefit. This tendency is related to temporal discounting: the subjective value of a reward generally decreases as the delay before receiving it increases. Differences in future-oriented imagination, uncertainty, and individual preferences can influence the strength of this effect.
Study instruction:
- Review temporal discounting and distinguish the objective amount of a reward from its subjective value at different points in time.
Key point:
- Immediate rewards often have greater psychological impact than delayed rewards.
- Delayed benefits may be discounted despite having a greater objective value.
Source links:
- Economic and Social Values in the Brain: Evidence from Lesions to the Human Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
- Characterization of the Decision-Making Deficit of Patients with Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex Lesions
29. Q: What is temporal discounting, and how does it influence everyday choices?
A: Show answer
Temporal discounting is the tendency to assign lower subjective value to rewards or outcomes as the time before they are received increases. A person may prefer receiving $100 today rather than $120 in one month, even though the delayed alternative offers a greater monetary amount. The same principle applies to health, education, saving, and career decisions. Someone may choose immediate entertainment instead of studying because the immediate benefit is more vivid than the future benefit of academic success. Temporal discounting is not necessarily irrational in every circumstance because delayed outcomes involve uncertainty, changing preferences, and opportunity costs. However, excessive discounting can undermine long-term objectives.
Study instruction:
- Compare immediate and delayed rewards using examples from saving, education, health, and personal financial decisions.
Key point:
- Delayed rewards are often subjectively discounted.
- Temporal discounting can create conflict between immediate preferences and long-term goals.
Source links:
- Economic and Social Values in the Brain: Evidence from Lesions to the Human Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
- Characterization of the Decision-Making Deficit of Patients with Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex Lesions
30. Q: How does imagining future rewards influence immediate decision-making?
A: Show answer
Imagining future rewards can make delayed outcomes more psychologically meaningful and improve the ability to consider their long-term benefits. Research by Todd Hare and colleagues examined participants imagining their preferred juice and water. Activity in the ventromedial prefrontal cortex was observed when participants imagined their preferred drink, although sensory-related activation differed from actually tasting it. The chapter describes an association between participants' choices involving immediate and delayed monetary rewards and their neural responses when imagining future rewards. Individuals who showed less activation in the ventromedial prefrontal cortex during imagined future rewards were more likely to select the immediate option. These findings suggest that the ability to represent future outcomes can influence decisions involving delayed gratification.
Study instruction:
- Review the distinction between imagining a reward and actually experiencing it, and explain how future-oriented representations can affect choices.
Key point:
- Imagining future rewards can influence their subjective value.
- Future-oriented evaluation is relevant to delayed gratification.
Source links:
- Chapter 6, Cognitive Neuroscience, Richard Passingham (supplied source).
- Economic and Social Values in the Brain: Evidence from Lesions to the Human Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
31. Q: Why do people sometimes underestimate future costs and risks?
A: Show answer
Future costs and risks are often underestimated because they are less immediate, less vivid, and sometimes uncertain. An immediate loss can produce a stronger psychological response than a potential loss that may occur months or years later. For example, someone may spend beyond their budget because the immediate enjoyment of purchasing an item is more salient than the future financial consequences. Similarly, a person may engage in risky behaviour because the immediate reward receives greater attention than the possibility of a delayed adverse outcome. The ability to imagine future consequences and incorporate them into present choices is therefore important for effective decision-making. However, uncertainty and probability must also be considered rather than assuming that every future risk will occur.
Study instruction:
- Identify examples in which immediate benefits compete with uncertain future costs, and explain how anticipating consequences can improve decisions.
Key point:
- Immediate outcomes can dominate less vivid future consequences.
- Effective decision-making requires evaluating both potential benefits and delayed costs.
Source links:
- Economic and Social Values in the Brain: Evidence from Lesions to the Human Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
- Characterization of the Decision-Making Deficit of Patients with Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex Lesions
32. Q: What is the Iowa Gambling Task, and what does it measure?
A: Show answer
The Iowa Gambling Task is a psychological assessment used to investigate decision-making under uncertainty and the ability to learn from rewards and losses. Participants repeatedly select cards from four decks. Some decks offer relatively large immediate gains but produce larger losses over time, while others provide smaller gains and more favourable long-term outcomes. Participants must gradually learn which choices are advantageous by observing the consequences of their selections. The task examines how individuals integrate previous outcomes, evaluate risk, and adjust subsequent choices. It has been widely used in research involving the ventromedial prefrontal cortex. However, performance is influenced by several cognitive and motivational processes, so it should not be interpreted as a pure measure of one isolated brain function.
Study instruction:
- Learn the difference between advantageous and disadvantageous decks and explain how repeated feedback influences subsequent choices.
Key point:
- The task assesses decision-making under uncertainty.
- Successful performance requires learning from the long-term consequences of choices.
Source links:
- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
- Economic and Social Values in the Brain: Evidence from Lesions to the Human Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
33. Q: Why do people sometimes continue choosing risky options despite repeated losses?
A: Show answer
People may continue choosing risky options because immediate rewards are attractive, while future losses are insufficiently represented or integrated into subsequent decisions. In the Iowa Gambling Task, disadvantageous decks can provide substantial immediate gains despite producing larger losses over time. Individuals who struggle to learn from the consequences of their decisions may repeatedly select these decks. Difficulties can involve evaluating delayed consequences, updating the subjective value of options, responding to negative feedback, or regulating reward-seeking behaviour. Ventromedial prefrontal dysfunction has been associated with impaired value-based decision-making, although risky choices cannot automatically be attributed to damage in this region alone.
Study instruction:
- Analyse why immediate rewards can maintain risky behaviour despite negative long-term outcomes.
Key point:
- Attractive immediate rewards can obscure cumulative future losses.
- Effective risk evaluation requires learning from previous outcomes.
Source links:
- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
- Economic and Social Values in the Brain: Evidence from Lesions to the Human Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
34. Q: What did research involving patients with ventromedial prefrontal cortex damage reveal about risky decision-making?
A: Show answer
Research by Antoine Bechara, Antonio Damasio, and colleagues demonstrated that patients with damage involving the ventromedial prefrontal cortex often performed poorly on the Iowa Gambling Task. They were more likely to select disadvantageous decks offering attractive immediate gains but larger long-term losses. The findings suggested that ventromedial prefrontal integrity contributes to integrating information about potential consequences into decisions. However, the results require careful interpretation. The task involves several cognitive processes, and some patients had extensive lesions affecting additional frontal regions. Consequently, poor performance cannot always be attributed exclusively to the ventromedial prefrontal cortex. Later research has examined more specific task conditions and the contribution of different neural regions.
Study instruction:
- Review the original lesion findings and identify the limitations of drawing conclusions from patients with extensive brain damage.
Key point:
- Ventromedial prefrontal damage can impair value-based risky decision-making.
- Lesion extent and task complexity must be considered when interpreting results.
Source links:
- Characterization of the Decision-Making Deficit of Patients with Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex Lesions
- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
35. Q: What is a skin conductance response, and why is it relevant to decision-making?
A: Show answer
A skin conductance response (SCR) is a measurable change in the electrical conductance of the skin associated with changes in sympathetic nervous system activity. It is commonly assessed through changes in the electrical properties of the skin, particularly on the palms. Emotional arousal, anticipation, and heightened alertness can produce changes in skin conductance. In decision-making research, SCRs can provide information about physiological responses that occur before or after a choice. In the Iowa Gambling Task, anticipatory SCRs have been studied as indicators of physiological responses associated with risky choices. They provide evidence that decision-making involves interactions between cognitive evaluation and bodily responses. However, SCRs do not directly reveal a person's precise thoughts or prove that a particular choice is consciously understood.
Study instruction:
- Understand how SCRs are measured and distinguish physiological arousal from conscious awareness of risk.
Key point:
- SCRs provide an objective measure of certain autonomic responses.
- Physiological signals can contribute to research on emotional and risk-related decision-making.
Source links:
- Characterization of the Decision-Making Deficit of Patients with Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex Lesions
- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
36. Q: What is the somatic marker hypothesis, and how does it relate to risky decisions?
A: Show answer
The somatic marker hypothesis proposes that bodily and emotional signals can help guide decision-making by providing information about the anticipated consequences of possible actions. These signals may arise when individuals consider or approach particular options and can influence their subsequent behaviour. In the context of the Iowa Gambling Task, anticipatory physiological responses have been interpreted as potential indicators of the expected consequences of disadvantageous choices. The hypothesis highlights the interaction between emotion, bodily responses, and cognitive evaluation. However, the interpretation of physiological signals remains debated, and researchers distinguish between unconscious physiological influences, conscious feelings, and explicit knowledge about the advantages or disadvantages of particular options.
Study instruction:
- Explain how emotional and physiological responses may contribute to decision-making without treating them as infallible indicators of correct choices.
Key point:
- Bodily responses can contribute to evaluating potential consequences.
- Emotional signals interact with cognitive processes during decision-making.
Source links:
- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
- Characterization of the Decision-Making Deficit of Patients with Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex Lesions
37. Q: How can pathological gambling be understood from the perspective of decision-making neuroscience?
A: Show answer
Pathological gambling involves persistent gambling behaviour despite significant negative consequences. From a decision-making perspective, it can involve excessive preference for immediate rewards, impaired evaluation of long-term losses, and difficulties adjusting behaviour after negative outcomes. The chapter discusses research indicating that pathological gamblers showed reduced anticipatory skin conductance responses before risky choices and greater risk-taking on the Iowa Gambling Task compared with non-gamblers. These findings suggest differences in the physiological and cognitive processes involved in anticipating negative consequences. However, gambling disorder is multifactorial and cannot be explained by one brain region or physiological measure. Learning, motivation, reward sensitivity, environmental influences, and psychological factors also contribute.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the behavioural features of problematic gambling from the neural mechanisms that may contribute to persistent risky choices.
Key point:
- Gambling disorder can involve impaired control over reward-seeking behaviour.
- Reduced consideration of future consequences may contribute to persistent risk-taking.
Source links:
- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
- Economic and Social Values in the Brain: Evidence from Lesions to the Human Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
38. Q: What is the relationship between empathy and moral reasoning?
A: Show answer
Empathy involves recognising and understanding another person's emotional experience and, in some circumstances, sharing or responding to that experience. Moral reasoning involves evaluating actions in relation to principles, obligations, rights, welfare, and potential consequences. Empathy can contribute to moral reasoning because understanding how an action affects another person provides information about possible harm, distress, or benefit. The principle of treating others as one would wish to be treated requires some ability to consider another person's perspective. However, empathy alone does not establish whether a moral principle is logically justified. Moral reasoning also involves social norms, rational evaluation, cultural influences, and ethical principles. The distinction between psychological mechanisms and moral justification is therefore important.
Study instruction:
- Compare empathy, perspective-taking, emotional concern, and moral reasoning, and explain how they interact without treating them as identical.
Key point:
- Empathy can inform moral judgement by highlighting effects on others.
- Moral principles require more than the existence of an emotional response.

- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
- Economic and Social Values in the Brain: Evidence from Lesions to the Human Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
39. Q: What is the difference between understanding another person's thoughts and understanding another person's feelings?
A: Show answer
Understanding another person's thoughts involves inferring their beliefs, intentions, knowledge, and expectations. Understanding another person's feelings involves recognising their emotional state and considering how a situation affects their emotional experience. Both processes contribute to social cognition, but they are not identical. For example, understanding that a colleague believes a meeting starts at 10 a.m. involves reasoning about that person's knowledge. Understanding that the colleague feels anxious because they are unprepared involves recognising their emotional experience. Research involving adolescents has examined these distinctions using cartoons requiring participants to infer either emotional reactions, thoughts, or physical consequences. Such research suggests that emotional and cognitive social understanding can involve overlapping but distinguishable neural processes.
Study instruction:
- Create examples that distinguish cognitive perspective-taking from emotional perspective-taking.
Key point:
- Understanding thoughts involves inferring mental states.
- Understanding feelings involves recognising emotional experiences.
Source links:
- The Multifaceted Role of Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex
40. Q: What did research conducted in Sarah-Jayne Blakemore's laboratory reveal about emotional understanding?
A: Show answer
Research conducted by a team in Sarah-Jayne Blakemore's laboratory studied boys aged approximately 11–16 using cartoons depicting interactions between people. Participants selected endings that required understanding emotional reactions, mental states, or physical causation. In the emotional condition, successful responses required recognising how a person would feel in a particular situation. In the mental-state condition, participants had to infer what someone thought or knew. A control condition involved understanding physical events. The findings showed activation in the ventromedial prefrontal cortex when participants interpreted emotional situations compared with physical causation. The comparison involving thoughts suggested a smaller effect that required further replication. The experiment demonstrated the importance of distinguishing emotional understanding from other forms of social reasoning.
Study instruction:
- Review the three experimental conditions and explain why the comparison with physical causation was important.
Key point:
- Emotional understanding can engage the ventromedial prefrontal cortex.
- Social cognition includes distinguishable emotional and cognitive processes.

41. Q: Why is the ability to imagine another person's emotional experience important for moral behaviour?
A: Show answer
Imagining another person's emotional experience helps individuals anticipate how their actions may affect that person. This can influence decisions about whether behaviour is considerate, harmful, inappropriate, or socially acceptable. For example, deciding whether to make a humiliating comment requires considering the likely emotional effect on the person receiving it. The ability to understand others' feelings can therefore support empathy, social cooperation, and compliance with moral expectations. The ventromedial prefrontal cortex contributes to aspects of emotional and social cognition, although moral behaviour involves multiple neural systems and broader social influences. Understanding another person's feelings does not automatically determine the correct moral action, but it provides information relevant to ethical judgement.
Study instruction:
- Apply emotional perspective-taking to everyday interpersonal situations and identify the likely consequences for the people involved.
Key point:
- Emotional perspective-taking supports consideration of other people's welfare.
- Empathy contributes to, but does not completely determine, moral judgement.
