Table 8
Motor Skills, Language Lateralization, and Prediction Errors
1. Q: Why is there a relation between handedness and the cerebral hemisphere that is specialized for language?
A: Show answer
The relationship probably reflects the evolutionary development of gestural communication before spoken language. Right-handedness is associated with greater involvement of the left cerebral hemisphere in skilled motor control. If early hominins used gestures to communicate, the left hemisphere's specialization for controlling the right hand may have provided an existing neural foundation for gestural communication. Over evolutionary time, this specialization could have supported the development of spoken language. Modern evidence supports this relationship: the left hemisphere, particularly Broca's area, contributes to both spoken and gestural language. The asymmetrical connections between Broca's area and other language-related regions further support the idea that the left hemisphere became specialized for communication.
Study instruction:
- Review the relationship between right-handedness, left-hemisphere specialization, gestural communication, and the evolution of spoken language.
Key point:
- Right-handedness is associated with left-hemisphere specialization.
- Gestural communication may have provided a pre-adaptation for spoken language.
2. Q: How is it that we can learn to play the violin?
A: Show answer
Learning to play the violin requires the development of precise, coordinated, and increasingly automatic movements. The fingers must move independently, apply appropriate force, and maintain accurate timing. Through repeated practice, the nervous system learns the relationship between movements and their consequences. The cerebellum plays an important role in acquiring and automating these skills. As performance becomes more automatic, the individual becomes less dependent on continuous external visual cues and increasingly relies on learned internal movement sequences. The cerebellum also helps predict the sensory consequences of movements and identify discrepancies between expected and actual outcomes. Successful violin performance therefore depends on coordinated motor control, practice, sensory feedback, prediction, and cerebellar learning mechanisms.
Study instruction:
- Explain how repetition, finger independence, timing, sensory feedback, and cerebellar mechanisms contribute to learning an instrumental skill.
Key point:
- Practice transforms controlled movements into increasingly automatic skills.
- The cerebellum contributes to motor learning, coordination, and prediction.
3. Q: Why can’t we tickle ourselves?
A: Show answer
We generally cannot tickle ourselves because the brain predicts the sensory consequences of our own movements. When we initiate an action, the nervous system generates an expectation about the sensations that should follow. If the actual sensory feedback matches the prediction, the sensation is less surprising and produces a weaker tickling response. When another person performs the same action, the movement and its sensory consequences are less predictable. This uncertainty makes the sensation more noticeable and potentially ticklish. Experiments involving robotic devices demonstrated that introducing a delay or changing the direction of self-generated stimulation increases ticklishness and cerebellar activation. This suggests that the cerebellum contributes to comparing predicted sensory consequences with actual sensory feedback.
Study instruction:
- Review the experiments involving robotic stimulation and explain how sensory prediction errors influence ticklishness.
Key point:
- Self-generated sensations are predictable and therefore less ticklish.
- Unexpected sensory feedback produces prediction errors.
4. Q: What are the principal differences between human hand control and that of other primates?
A: Show answer
Humans demonstrate three particularly important characteristics. First, they have a high degree of independent finger control, enabling complex activities such as playing musical instruments. Second, most humans show a preference for using the right hand in skilled activities such as writing and throwing. Third, handedness is associated with the cerebral hemisphere specialized for language. Although individual chimpanzees may prefer one hand for particular tasks, wild chimpanzee populations do not demonstrate the same consistent population-level right-handedness observed in humans. These differences reflect important developments in human motor organization and communication.
Study instruction:
- Compare human finger dexterity, population-level handedness, and language lateralization with the characteristics described for other primates.
Key point:
- Independent finger control supports complex human motor skills.
- Human handedness is associated with cerebral specialization for language.
5. Q: What is handedness, and how does it influence skilled motor performance?
A: Show answer
Handedness refers to an individual's tendency to prefer one hand when performing skilled actions. Most humans prefer their right hand for activities requiring precision, including writing, throwing, and manipulating tools. Hand preference allows an individual to develop consistent motor strategies through repeated practice. Using the same hand repeatedly also allows the same contralateral cerebral hemisphere to acquire and refine the relevant motor skills. This specialization can improve efficiency, coordination, and consistency.
Study instruction:
- Identify activities that demonstrate handedness and explain why consistent use of one hand may facilitate motor learning.
Key point:
- Handedness reflects a preference for one hand in skilled activities.
- Consistent practice supports the development of specialized motor control.
6. Q: What archaeological evidence suggests that right-handedness developed early in human evolution?
A: Show answer
Archaeological evidence from stone tools suggests that the tendency towards right-handedness may extend approximately 1.5 million years into human evolution. When flakes were struck from stone cores, the direction of the strikes produced distinguishable marks. Researchers can examine these patterns to infer which hand was probably used. This evidence provides an indication that preferential hand use existed long before the emergence of modern spoken language.
Study instruction:
- Review how stone-tool production can provide indirect evidence of ancient handedness and distinguish archaeological evidence from direct observation.
Key point:
- Stone-tool markings provide evidence of early preferential hand use.
- Right-handedness probably predates spoken language.
7. Q: Why might consistent use of one hand provide an evolutionary advantage?
A: Show answer
Skilled activities such as stone-tool production require considerable practice. Repeatedly using the same hand allows the corresponding contralateral hemisphere to acquire and refine the necessary motor sequences. This consistency can improve the precision and efficiency of tool production. The individual benefits from developing a reliable motor strategy rather than repeatedly switching between hands and requiring different motor systems to perform the same skilled activity.
Study instruction:
- Explain how repeated practice using the same hand can strengthen specialized motor performance and identify the corresponding cerebral hemisphere.
Key point:
- Consistent hand use supports specialized motor learning.
- The cerebral hemisphere opposite the preferred hand is particularly involved.
8. Q: Why does the archaeological evidence for right-handedness not fully explain the development of population-level handedness?
A: Show answer
Evidence that individuals used a preferred hand does not explain why different individuals within a population developed similar preferences. The development of consistent population-level handedness requires an explanation for the similarity between individuals. One possibility discussed in the chapter is that shared instruction in stone-tool production or the repeated use of the same stone cores encouraged standardization. Such practices could have reinforced similar patterns of hand use across generations. These explanations are possibilities rather than definitive demonstrations of the evolutionary mechanism.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish evidence of individual hand preference from explanations for the emergence of population-level handedness.
Key point:
- Individual preference does not automatically explain population-wide patterns.
- Shared practices may have contributed to standardization.
9. Q: What is the relationship between the evolution of handedness and the development of spoken language?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that handedness probably developed before spoken language. The estimated emergence of speech is approximately 70,000–100,000 years ago, whereas archaeological evidence suggests that preferential hand use may extend much further into the past. This chronological difference supports the possibility that an existing left-hemisphere specialization for skilled manual activity was subsequently adapted for communication. If early humans used gestures before developing spoken language, the neural organization involved in controlling the dominant hand may have contributed to the development of specialized gestural communication systems.
Study instruction:
- Compare the estimated evolutionary timelines of right-handedness and spoken language and explain the significance of their relationship.
Key point:
- Handedness probably preceded spoken language.
- Existing motor specialization may have facilitated communication development.
10. Q: What is meant by the idea that gestural communication provided a pre-adaptation for spoken language?
A: Show answer
A pre-adaptation refers to an existing biological structure or function that subsequently becomes useful for a different evolutionary purpose. In this context, the proposal is that the left hemisphere initially became specialized for skilled right-hand movements and potentially gestural communication. When spoken language developed, existing neural structures and connections involved in communication could have provided a foundation for speech specialization. This explanation suggests that language-related neural systems developed partly through the modification of existing capabilities rather than appearing entirely as new mechanisms.
Study instruction:
- Be able to explain pre-adaptation using the relationship between hand control, gestures, and spoken language.
Key point:
- Pre-adaptation involves repurposing existing biological capabilities.
- Gestural communication may have contributed to the evolution of speech mechanisms.
11. Q: What evidence indicates that the left hemisphere is important for gestural communication?
A: Show answer
Research by Ursula Bellugi involving users of American Sign Language demonstrated that left-hemisphere lesions can severely impair the production and comprehension of gestures. Comparable right-hemisphere lesions do not produce the same degree of impairment. Importantly, the difficulties cannot simply be explained by general motor or sensory loss. Some patients produce incorrect gestures when naming objects, selecting gestures appropriate to different objects. This suggests that the left hemisphere contributes to the linguistic organization and meaningful interpretation of gestures rather than merely controlling hand movements.
Study instruction:
- Review Bellugi's findings and explain why incorrect gesture selection is evidence of a communication impairment rather than simply a motor impairment.
Key point:
- The left hemisphere contributes to gestural language.
- Gestural communication involves more than basic hand movement.
12. Q: How does American Sign Language research help distinguish language impairment from motor impairment?
A: Show answer
American Sign Language research demonstrates that individuals can experience difficulty producing meaningful gestures even when they retain the ability to move their hands. A patient who selects an incorrect gesture when identifying an object may have a problem retrieving or organizing the appropriate linguistic representation rather than a basic motor deficit. This distinction is important because it shows that gestural language depends on specialized cognitive and neural mechanisms. The ability to execute a movement is not equivalent to the ability to use that movement as a meaningful linguistic expression.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish motor execution problems from impairments involving the selection and organization of meaningful gestures.
Key point:
- Motor ability and linguistic gesture ability are different functions.
- Gesture production requires meaningful communication processes.
13. Q: What did Karen Emmorey's brain-imaging research reveal about Broca's area and American Sign Language?
A: Show answer
Emmorey used positron emission tomography (PET) to investigate brain activity in hearing individuals performing sign-language and spoken-language tasks. When participants named objects using American Sign Language or speech, both activities activated the left Broca's area. This finding indicates that Broca's area participates in processes common to both forms of language production. However, understanding signs and understanding spoken words produced differences in activation, particularly involving the left inferior parietal cortex during observation of gestures.
Study instruction:
- Compare the brain-imaging findings for producing spoken language, producing sign language, and understanding gestures.
Key point:
- Broca's area is involved in both spoken and signed language production.
- Different language processes can recruit additional specialized brain regions.
14. Q: What role does the left inferior parietal cortex play in gestural communication?
A: Show answer
The left inferior parietal cortex contributes to processing information about biological motion and interpreting observed gestures. Research described in the chapter found activation in this region when individuals understood signs and judged whether gestures were the same or different. This region therefore contributes to analyzing the movement patterns that carry communicative meaning. Its connections with Broca's area provide a potential pathway through which information about gestures can contribute to language-related processing.
Study instruction:
- Review the relationship between biological-motion processing, gesture recognition, and the inferior parietal cortex.
Key point:
- The inferior parietal cortex contributes to gesture processing.
- It is anatomically connected to Broca's area.
15. Q: Why are the connections between the inferior parietal cortex and Broca's area important?
A: Show answer
The anterior part of the inferior parietal cortex connects directly to Broca's area. This connection is important because the inferior parietal cortex processes information associated with gestures, while Broca's area participates in language-related processing. Their connection provides a potential neural pathway through which gestural information can influence the systems involved in communication. Diffusion-weighted imaging has shown that this fibre tract is more extensive in the left hemisphere than in the right, supporting the existence of structural asymmetry in human communication networks.
Study instruction:
- Identify the two connected regions and explain how their functions contribute to gestural communication.
Key point:
- The inferior parietal cortex provides gesture-related information to Broca's area.
- Left-hemisphere connectivity is more extensive.
16. Q: What is the significance of the asymmetrical fibre connections in the human brain?
A: Show answer
Asymmetrical fibre connections indicate that communication-related neural networks are not organized identically in both cerebral hemispheres. The chapter describes more extensive connections involving Broca's area in the left hemisphere, including connections with the inferior parietal cortex and Wernicke's area. This structural organization may support the integration of information about gestures and speech sounds. The absence of comparable asymmetry in chimpanzees suggests that these connectivity patterns may represent an important development in human brain evolution.
Study instruction:
- Compare left- and right-hemisphere connectivity and explain why structural asymmetry may support specialized communication functions.
Key point:
- Human communication networks demonstrate left-hemisphere structural specialization.
- Neural connectivity contributes to the integration of different communication processes.
17. Q: How do Broca's area and Wernicke's area contribute to language processing?
A: Show answer
Broca's area is associated particularly with the production of spoken language, while Wernicke's area is associated particularly with language comprehension. In right-handed individuals, damage to left Broca's area can impair speech production, whereas damage to left Wernicke's area can impair comprehension. The two regions are interconnected, allowing information associated with language sounds and linguistic processing to be integrated. The chapter also emphasizes that Broca's area has connections with systems involved in gestural communication, supporting its broader role in communication.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish speech production from language comprehension and explain the importance of connectivity between their associated brain regions.
Key point:
- Broca's area is particularly important for speech production.
- Wernicke's area is particularly important for language comprehension.
18. Q: What is the relationship between Broca's area, apraxia, and aphasia?
A: Show answer
Aphasia refers to an impairment of language, whereas apraxia refers to difficulty performing learned purposeful actions or gestures despite the absence of a simple explanation in terms of weakness or basic motor loss. The chapter describes research by Gereon Fink and colleagues involving 50 patients with left-hemisphere strokes. Approximately 75% experienced language difficulties, and 47% experienced problems imitating gestures. Both groups showed similarities in damage involving Broca's area, although patients with gestural difficulties were more likely to have additional damage in the inferior parietal cortex and ventral prefrontal cortex. These findings suggest that Broca's area may act as a common hub for gestural and vocal communication systems.
Study instruction:
- Memorize the distinction between aphasia and apraxia and review the findings from the 50-patient lesion study.
Key point:
- Aphasia involves language impairment.
- Apraxia involves impaired execution of learned purposeful actions.
- Broca's area may contribute to both gestural and vocal communication.
19. Q: What did Fink and colleagues discover about patients with left-hemisphere lesions?
A: Show answer
Fink and colleagues compared lesion patterns in 50 patients who had suffered left-hemisphere strokes. Seventy-five percent experienced language difficulties, while 47% had difficulty imitating gestures. Although the groups showed differences in the precise locations of additional damage, both demonstrated involvement of Broca's area. Patients with gesture imitation difficulties were more likely to have damage in the inferior parietal cortex and ventral prefrontal cortex. These findings support the possibility that Broca's area is a shared neural hub for systems involved in gestural and vocal communication.
Study instruction:
- Review the sample size, reported percentages, affected regions, and implications for shared communication networks.
Key point:
- The study identified overlapping lesion patterns for gestural and language impairments.
- Broca's area may support both communication systems.
20. Q: Why does the evidence suggest that gestural communication may have preceded spoken language?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that the asymmetry of the neural connections involved in gestural communication is difficult to explain as a consequence of spoken language alone. Since right-handedness probably developed before speech, left-hemisphere motor specialization may have provided an earlier foundation for gestural communication. The existing neural connections could then have supported the later development of spoken language. This interpretation is consistent with evidence showing that Broca's area participates in both gestural and vocal communication.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate the evolutionary argument by linking archaeological evidence, cerebral asymmetry, and modern language research.
Key point:
- Gestural communication may have preceded spoken language.
- Existing motor and communication networks may have been adapted for speech.
21. Q: What advantage does spoken language have over gestural communication?
A: Show answer
Both gestural and spoken communication can assign names to objects and convey information. However, spoken language provides greater flexibility for generating an effectively unlimited variety of sentences. It can communicate complex relationships and abstract ideas through combinations of words organized according to grammatical rules. This flexibility provides an important advantage over communication systems that depend primarily on visible gestures, particularly when communication needs to express complex information efficiently.
Study instruction:
- Compare the communicative flexibility of spoken and gestural systems and identify the advantages of sentence generation.
Key point:
- Spoken language supports highly flexible sentence generation.
- Grammatical organization allows complex communication.
22. Q: What is hierarchical organization in language?
A: Show answer
Hierarchical organization refers to the arrangement of linguistic elements into structured relationships rather than simply placing words in a linear sequence. Words and phrases can form nested structures in which elements are related across different levels of a sentence. This organization allows language to express complex grammatical relationships. The chapter discusses the proposal that specialized neural mechanisms support the learning and processing of these hierarchical structures, particularly involving Broca's area.
Study instruction:
- Practice distinguishing simple adjacent sequences from hierarchical sequences containing nested relationships and long-distance dependencies.
Key point:
- Language involves structured relationships, not merely sequences of words.
- Hierarchical organization supports complex grammatical expression.
23. Q: What was Noam Chomsky's argument concerning the evolution of language mechanisms?
A: Show answer
Chomsky argued that the ability to generate grammatical sentences depends on a hierarchical system rather than simply combining words into sequences. He proposed that the mechanism responsible for this capacity evolved de novo, meaning that it did not emerge through the gradual adaptation of previously existing mechanisms for processing sequences. This proposal raises an important evolutionary question: whether the human brain possesses specialized mechanisms for hierarchical language processing or whether general neural mechanisms can be adapted to perform this function.
Study instruction:
- Explain Chomsky's position and distinguish a newly evolved specialized mechanism from the adaptation of general-purpose neural mechanisms.
Key point:
- Chomsky proposed that hierarchical language mechanisms evolved de novo.
- Alternative explanations emphasize the adaptation of existing neural capabilities.
24. Q: How did Angela Friederici and colleagues investigate hierarchical language processing?
A: Show answer
Friederici and colleagues developed artificial grammars to investigate how the brain processes different types of structured sequences. One grammar involved local dependencies between adjacent elements, while another involved hierarchical relationships and nested structures. Participants learned both systems and subsequently performed grammaticality judgments while undergoing brain imaging. The researchers found greater activation in several brain regions for hierarchical processing, including the left Broca's area. However, the findings raised an important interpretative issue because hierarchical sequences also required participants to maintain information in temporary memory over longer intervals.
Study instruction:
- Review the experimental design, the differences between the two artificial grammars, and the interpretation of increased Broca's area activation.
Key point:
- Artificial grammars can investigate neural mechanisms of sequence learning.
- Hierarchical processing produced increased activation in Broca's area.
25. Q: What are local dependencies and long-distance dependencies in sequence processing?
A: Show answer
Local dependencies involve relationships between nearby elements in a sequence. Long-distance dependencies involve relationships between elements separated by intervening material. Hierarchical language structures can contain such distant relationships because the interpretation of one element may depend on another element appearing earlier in the sequence. Processing long-distance relationships may require information to remain temporarily available in memory. This makes it necessary to distinguish the cognitive demands of remembering earlier elements from the demands of integrating them into grammatical structures.
Study instruction:
- Construct examples of adjacent and separated relationships and explain why long-distance dependencies increase memory demands.
Key point:
- Local dependencies involve nearby elements.
- Long-distance dependencies require integration across intervening elements.
26. Q: Why was the interpretation of increased Broca's area activation in Friederici's experiment not straightforward?
A: Show answer
The hierarchical grammar required participants to maintain earlier syllables in temporary memory while processing later elements. Consequently, increased activation in Broca's area might have reflected the memory demands associated with maintaining information rather than a specialized mechanism for processing hierarchical relationships. The experiment therefore could not conclusively establish whether the activation resulted from hierarchical structure itself or from the additional cognitive demands associated with longer-distance dependencies.
Study instruction:
- Identify potential confounding variables in experimental research and explain why memory demands can complicate interpretations of brain-imaging results.
Key point:
- Increased brain activation does not automatically identify a specific cognitive function.
- Experimental controls are necessary to distinguish competing explanations.
27. Q: How did Karl Magnus Petersson and Peter Hagoort investigate the relationship between memory and hierarchical sequence processing?
A: Show answer
Petersson and Hagoort compared artificial grammars designed to control for familiarity with adjacent and non-adjacent sequences. Participants were scanned while making grammaticality judgments involving hierarchical sequences. The researchers found activation in the region of Broca's area regardless of whether the relevant dependencies were local or distant. Their findings helped address the possibility that Broca's area activation in earlier experiments was explained solely by the memory requirements associated with long-distance dependencies.
Study instruction:
- Compare this experimental design with Friederici's approach and explain how controlling familiarity helps improve experimental interpretation.
Key point:
- Broca's area activation was observed for both local and distant dependencies.
- Experimental matching helps distinguish memory demands from sequence-processing demands.
28. Q: What did Petersson and Hagoort's research suggest about the role of Broca's area in sequence processing?
A: Show answer
Their findings suggest that Broca's area participates in processing structured sequences and is not exclusively activated by long-distance dependencies. Activation occurred when participants evaluated hierarchical sequences involving both local and distant relationships. This supports a broader interpretation in which Broca's area contributes to organizing and integrating sequence information. It does not, by itself, establish that Broca's area contains an exclusively language-specific grammatical mechanism.
Study instruction:
- Explain the distinction between evidence for sequence-processing involvement and evidence for a uniquely language-specific mechanism.
Key point:
- Broca's area participates in structured sequence processing.
- Its contribution may extend beyond processing long-distance dependencies.
29. Q: How can computational models help explain the evolution of language mechanisms?
A: Show answer
Computational models can simulate how neural systems learn structured sequences and help researchers determine whether the underlying mechanisms need to be specialized exclusively for language. By constructing models capable of learning artificial grammars, researchers can investigate whether general mechanisms for forming and storing associations over distance are sufficient to support hierarchical processing. If such mechanisms can learn multiple types of structured sequences, there may be no need to assume that specialized syntax mechanisms evolved entirely de novo.
Study instruction:
- Review how computational models test evolutionary explanations and distinguish general-purpose learning mechanisms from specialized language mechanisms.
Key point:
- Computational models help evaluate alternative explanations of language evolution.
- General sequence-learning mechanisms may contribute to grammatical processing.
30. Q: What did the neurobiological model developed by Petersson and Hagoort demonstrate?
A: Show answer
Their model incorporated mechanisms for forming and storing associations over distance. It was capable of learning artificial grammars and other tasks involving structured sequences. Its ability to perform different sequence-processing tasks suggested that the mechanisms underlying hierarchical processing need not be exclusively dedicated to syntax. Instead, general neural mechanisms for learning relationships between elements over time may contribute to the processing of language and other structured activities.
Study instruction:
- Explain the model's underlying mechanisms and identify why its ability to learn multiple tasks is important for evolutionary theories of language.

31. Q: What is a motor skill, and what characteristics distinguish skilled performance from ordinary movement?
A: Show answer
A motor skill is a learned ability to perform movements accurately and efficiently. Skilled performance requires precise control over the application of force, movement timing, rhythm, and coordination between different body parts. Activities such as playing the violin illustrate these requirements because the fingers must perform independent movements while maintaining appropriate force and timing. With practice, these movements become increasingly coordinated and automatic.
Study instruction:
- Identify the components of skilled motor performance and apply them to activities such as playing an instrument, writing, and sports.
Key point:
- Skilled performance requires precision, coordination, and timing.

32. Q: How does repeated practice transform a controlled movement into an automatic skill?
A: Show answer
Repeated practice allows the nervous system to learn consistent relationships between movements and their consequences. Initially, individuals may rely heavily on external feedback, such as visual instructions or cues. As the sequence becomes familiar, the nervous system develops internal associations that allow one movement to serve as a cue for the next. Performance consequently becomes less dependent on conscious monitoring and external guidance. The cerebellum contributes to this process by supporting the learning and coordination of movement sequences.
Study instruction:
- Compare early-stage and advanced-stage performance of a newly learned motor sequence and identify changes in external cue dependence.
Key point:
- Repetition strengthens learned movement associations.
- Automatic performance reduces reliance on external guidance.
33. Q: What did Anna Floyer-Lea and Paul Matthews discover about motor learning and cerebellar activation?
A: Show answer
Floyer-Lea and Matthews taught participants to apply a repeating sequence of forces using their forefinger to track a moving bar on a screen. As participants learned the sequence, they became less dependent on the visual display because the movements became predictable. Each force could act as a cue for the next movement. The researchers observed a marked increase in cerebellar activation as the sequence became learned and automatic. This finding supports the role of the cerebellum in acquiring and coordinating learned motor sequences.
Study instruction:
- Review the experimental task, the development of automatic performance, and the observed changes in cerebellar activity.
Key point:
- Learned motor sequences become less dependent on continuous visual guidance.
- Cerebellar activation increases during the learning of the sequence described in the study.
34. Q: What did Narender Ramnani's research reveal about the relationship between rhythm learning and visual cues?
A: Show answer
Ramnani trained participants to move a finger according to a particular rhythm. Initially, visual cues provided the timing needed to maintain the rhythm. As participants learned the sequence, their movements became less dependent on these external cues. Brain imaging showed decreasing activation in visual areas and increasing activation in the cerebellum. This pattern suggests that practice allows the nervous system to develop an internal representation of the movement sequence, reducing the need for continuous visual guidance.
Study instruction:
- Explain the changes in visual and cerebellar activation during the transition from externally guided to internally guided rhythmic movement.
Key point:
- Practice reduces dependence on external visual pacing.
- The cerebellum contributes to internally guided rhythmic performance.
35. Q: What role does the cerebellum play in the development of automatic motor skills?
A: Show answer
The cerebellum contributes to learning, coordinating, and automating skilled movement sequences. During initial learning, individuals often depend on external instructions and feedback. With practice, the cerebellum becomes increasingly involved in supporting learned sequences that can be performed with less conscious attention and less dependence on external cues. Its role extends beyond basic movement execution because it also helps predict the sensory consequences of actions and identify discrepancies between expected and actual feedback.
Study instruction:
- Connect the cerebellar findings from the force-sequence, rhythmic-movement, and sensory-prediction experiments.
Key point:
- The cerebellum contributes to motor learning and automaticity.
- It also supports prediction and error detection.
36. Q: How does the cerebellum contribute to language skills?
A: Show answer
The cerebellum contributes to the development and performance of language-related skills, particularly when speech production involves learned and repeated sequences. The chapter describes research by Marcus Raichle and Steve Petersen in which participants were asked to generate appropriate verbs in response to nouns. As the same nouns were repeated, participants increasingly produced predictable sequences of verbs. This development of repeated verbal sequences was associated with increased cerebellar activation. The findings suggest that cerebellar mechanisms involved in learning and automating sequences also contribute to language-related performance.

- Cerebellar involvement is not limited to manual movements.

37. Q: What is dysarthria, and how is it related to cerebellar damage?
A: Show answer
Dysarthria is a disorder involving impaired speech articulation. Damage to the cerebellum can interfere with the precise coordination and timing of movements required for speech production. The chapter explains that patients with cerebellar lesions may experience difficulties with both manual coordination and accurate articulation. These impairments demonstrate that the cerebellum contributes to the precision of movements involved in both motor and vocal performance.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish dysarthria from language comprehension difficulties and explain how impaired movement coordination can affect speech production.
